- [x] **Conclusion: 0/89 is correct behavior on this dataset, not a discoverability bug.** Plan 18's Fix 1 (rewording `find_implementations`'s description to explicitly welcome read-only enumeration questions) was a reasonable bet given the evidence available at the time (1/30 baseline dropping to 0/30 post-fix looked like a regression) — but this full-question-set read shows the "1/30" baseline itself was almost certainly noise around a true near-zero rate on this specific dataset, not a real behavioral signal the fix then suppressed. **No further tool-layer action taken — there's nothing to fix here.** Tweaking the description further, or anything at the retrieval/rerank layer, cannot move usage on a benchmark that structurally never asks the question this tool answers.
- [ ] Not started, and not decided without asking first (same caution as the Q26 task-file retraction): the only way to get *real* evidence on `find_implementations`'s quality would be either (a) a genuinely new benchmark task shaped like "what implements X" — additive, not editing any existing gold answer, so a different situation from the Q26 SOT concern — or (b) testing against a different, more inheritance-heavy corpus (e.g. a Java/TS codebase with real interface hierarchies, or even codegraph's own Rust workspace: `QueryPipeline`, `EmbeddingModel`, `LanguageSupport` are all real trait boundaries with multiple impls). Neither attempted; flagging both options rather than picking one unilaterally.
- [x] **Group F noise-floor repeat run and the two just-shipped Finding C/D fixes' own benchmark validation**: not "why hasn't this happened" mysteries — both are simply pending an explicit go-ahead for a paid run, per this session's own established gating convention (every paid harness run this session required an explicit "yes, run it" first). No investigation needed; just a decision point.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-04 Group F 3-repeat run (real noise-floor signal, at last)

*User asked to actually run the repeats. The single-run Group F pass earlier (checklist entry above) proved noise dominates at n=1 but couldn't say anything about the true ranking. Ran 2 more independent rounds (5 arms × 5 tasks each, fresh output dirs `claude-sonnet-5-groupf-repeat2`/`-repeat3` so nothing overwrites) — the existing single run counts as round 1 — for 3 total repeats, ~$11.83 for the 2 new rounds.*

- [x] **Per-arm Group F mean across 3 independent rounds** (`results/hard/claude-code/claude-sonnet-5-{fixed,groupf-repeat2,groupf-repeat3}/grades.json`):

  | arm | run1 | run2 | run3 | mean | stdev | range |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | codegraph | 73.5% | 64.0% | 76.5% | **71.3%** | 6.5pp | 12.5pp |
  | codegraph-colby | 74.0% | 67.5% | 71.0% | **70.8%** | 3.3pp | 6.5pp |
  | code-review-graph | 55.5% | 61.5% | 67.5% | 61.5% | 6.0pp | 12.0pp |
  | graphify | 52.5% | **80.0%** | 48.5% | 60.3% | **17.1pp** | **31.5pp** |
  | codegraph-nosem | 55.5% | 49.5% | 59.5% | 54.8% | 5.0pp | 10.0pp |

- [x] **Real signal, not just noise, at n=3**: `codegraph` (71.3%) and `codegraph-colby` (70.8%) are statistically indistinguishable from each other (0.5pp apart, well inside either's own spread) but both sit clearly above `code-review-graph` (61.5%), `graphify` (60.3%), and `codegraph-nosem` (54.8%) — roughly a 10-17pp gap, larger than the ~5-7pp stdev of the tightly-clustered arms. This is the first time in this session's Group F investigation that a ranking claim is backed by more than one data point per arm.
- [x] **Caveat worth keeping**: `graphify`'s 17.1pp stdev (driven by one 80.0% outlier run against two ~50% runs) is itself notable — that arm is far less stable on this 5-question slice than any other, so its 60.3% mean carries much less confidence than `codegraph`/`code-review-graph`/`codegraph-nosem`'s tighter spreads. 3 repeats narrows this a lot but doesn't fully resolve it; a 4th+ round would specifically help pin down whether graphify's true rate is closer to 50% or 60-70%.
- [x] **What this does and doesn't say about this session's own fixes**: `codegraph`'s 71.3% mean is consistent with — but still not proof of — the Q26/Q30 `resolve_symbol`/`trace_impact` fixes helping, for the same reason stated in the single-run entry above: `codegraph-colby` (untouched by any fix this session) lands in the same statistical range. What's now better-supported is the *relative* claim "codegraph and colby are the two strongest arms on this trap-question group," not a causal claim about which specific code change produced it.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-07 local-model provider added: Ollama via a revived LiteLLM proxy

*User asked whether the harness could drive a custom/local model (Ollama) through `claude` CLI for the benchmark, the same way `--provider deepseek` already does. Unlike DeepSeek, Ollama has no native Anthropic-Messages-API-compatible endpoint, so the LiteLLM translation proxy this repo had previously retired (obsoleted by DeepSeek's V4 native endpoint) needed to come back — for Ollama specifically, not DeepSeek.*

- [x] **`harness/proxy/config.yaml`** (new) — LiteLLM proxy config exposing LiteLLM's unified `/v1/messages` (Anthropic-shaped) route, routing `model_name: ollama-qwen` to `ollama_chat/qwen3.5:4b` (not the plain `ollama/` provider — `ollama_chat/` is required for LiteLLM to translate Ollama's native chat/tool-call format correctly). `general_settings.master_key: os.environ/LITELLM_MASTER_KEY` gates the proxy behind a static bearer token.
- [x] **`harness/providers.yaml`** — new `ollama-qwen` entry (`base_url: http://localhost:4000`, `api_key_env: LITELLM_MASTER_KEY`, all-zero pricing since nothing behind the proxy is a paid API); corrected two stale comments in the file header while touching it — the "LiteLLM proxy is obsolete" claim (false again now, just for a different provider) and an `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` vs `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` mismatch that had drifted from `run.py`'s actual (correct) behavior.
- [x] **README.md "Local model via Ollama" + `PIPELINE.md`** — new subsection with full setup (`ollama pull`, pinned `litellm[proxy]==1.100.0` install, start proxy, curl smoke test, harness smoke test), a security note pinning past PyPI supply-chain-compromised versions 1.82.7/1.82.8 (verified via web search — malicious versions were live ~3 hours in March 2026 before PyPI quarantined them; 1.82.6 and earlier predate it, current 1.9x+ postdates the cleanup), and a note that a $0.00 ledger here is *expected* (unlike the DeepSeek section, where it signals broken usage reporting).
- [x] **Verified live, not just documented**: installed `litellm[proxy]==1.100.0` and hit a real bug doing so — it hard-crashes with `ImportError: cannot import name 'NotRequired' from 'typing'` on Python 3.10 (this machine's default `python3`/`pip`), because litellm 1.100.0 imports `typing.NotRequired` directly with no `typing_extensions` fallback (stdlib-only since Python 3.11). Fixed by documenting an explicit 3.11+ interpreter via a venv (`/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.13 -m venv harness/proxy/.venv`) rather than the ambient `pip install`; added `harness/proxy/.venv/` to `.gitignore`. Re-verified clean install and startup under 3.13.
- [x] **Confirmed the proxy itself works end-to-end** via raw `curl -X POST /v1/messages`: a plain completion round-trips correctly (real `usage.input_tokens`/`output_tokens`, actual model text), and — the case that actually matters, since 3 of 5 benchmark arms are tool-call-dependent — a tool-definition request correctly returns a `tool_use` block (`stop_reason: "tool_use"`, well-formed `input` JSON) from qwen3.5:4b through the proxy.
- [x] **Ran one real harness smoke test** (`run.py --arm baseline --task Q01 --provider ollama-qwen`, no MCP tools, `--allowedTools Grep,Read,Glob` only) — exit 0, but the model itself did not converge: `num_turns: 25` (hit the cap), `final_text` empty, and — the more interesting finding — `tool_names: {'Grep': 5, 'TaskList': 17, 'ListAgents': 2}`. 19 of 24 tool-call attempts were for `TaskList`/`ListAgents`, tools that were never in this arm's `--allowedTools` at all. qwen3.5:4b appears to hallucinate tool names outside what the CLI actually exposed to it, a different (and worse) failure mode than DeepSeek's turn-inefficiency-but-real-tools pattern documented earlier in this file. Documented in README as an n=1 observation, not a generalized verdict — but flagged that any local-model run's `tool_names` should be checked before trusting its coverage score, since a hallucinated-tool run can rack up `tool_calls` without ever touching the actual MCP surface being benchmarked.
- [x] **Not hallucination — a real tool-registry drift bug, general to the whole harness**: the "`TaskList`/`ListAgents` calls on a no-MCP arm" pattern above turned out not to be model hallucination. Claude Code's CLI (`claude_code_version: 2.1.259`, seen in the transcript's `init` event) split its old `Task`/`Agent` tools into `TaskCreate`/`TaskGet`/`TaskList`/`TaskUpdate`/`ListAgents`, and `run.py`'s exhaustive `--disallowedTools` list (the mechanism the harness's own comments call load-bearing for the "Grep/Read/Glob + one MCP server" fairness floor, since `--allowedTools` only *adds* to Claude Code's full default toolset) still only named the old ones. This means **every arm on every provider** — not just `ollama-qwen` — was leaking these 5 tools past the intended floor. Fixed: added `TaskCreate`, `TaskGet`, `TaskList`, `TaskUpdate`, `ListAgents` to `run.py`'s `disallowed` list. Re-verified live: a re-run of the same Q01/Q02 baseline questions after the fix showed only `Grep`/`Read`/`Glob` in `tool_names`, zero Task*/ListAgents calls.
- [x] **Second real bug: `num_ctx`, not thinking itself, was blocking every answer.** User explicitly directed thinking must stay enabled — correctly, since disabling it would change what's being benchmarked. With thinking on, qwen3.5:4b's `<thinking>` output didn't just run long, it **never terminated** — confirmed by raising `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS` from 32000 all the way to 128000 and getting the identical `"API Error: ... exceeded the ... output token maximum"` failure at the new ceiling too, i.e. more budget alone didn't fix it. Root-caused via `ollama show qwen3.5:4b` (262144 native context) vs. Ollama's small server-side default `num_ctx` when a caller doesn't request more (confirmed via `ollama ps`, no `OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH` override and no `num_ctx` in the model's own Modelfile params) — once the model fills its default (small) context window mid-`<thinking>`, it degenerates into non-terminating repetition instead of concluding. Fixed by setting `num_ctx: 32768` in `harness/proxy/config.yaml`'s `litellm_params` (LiteLLM passes this straight through to Ollama's chat API). Verified via `ollama ps` (CONTEXT column actually shows 32768, confirming the override took effect, not just accepted-and-ignored) and a raw `/v1/messages` curl call, which went from never finishing at a 128000-token cap to a clean `end_turn` in ~7s at the default 32000-token cap once `num_ctx` was raised.
- [x] **Full 5-task `baseline` arm run after both fixes**: 3/5 (Q01, Q02, Q05) produced real, substantive, on-topic final answers with `rc: 0` — a genuine result, not just plumbing working. Q03 still hit the 32000-output-token ceiling (not yet re-tried with a higher `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS`) and Q04 came back empty. Real cost of the `num_ctx` fix: wall-clock exploded to 460-1623s/task (vs. single-digit seconds for the cloud providers in the DeepSeek section), since a much larger context window means far more GPU work per token — and this was still only the tool-free `baseline` arm.
- [ ] **Not done**: `codegraph`/`graphify`/`code-review-graph` (the 3 real-MCP-tool arms) are completely untested against `ollama-qwen` — everything above is `baseline` only. Q03/Q04's remaining failures haven't been re-attempted with a higher output-token ceiling. No full 30-question run. Given the ~10-27 min/task wall-clock observed, a full 5-arm×30-question run would take a very long time; a smaller stratified sample would be the more realistic next step if this is pursued further.
- [x] **Graded the 5-task `baseline` run** (`harness/grade.py` + `report.py` against `results/hard/claude-code/ollama-qwen`): mean coverage **3.3%** (Q01 16.7% partial credit, Q02-Q05 all 0%). Confirms the fluency-without-correctness read above with hard evidence, not just impression — Q02's `rc: 0` "success" was graded 0/7 facts because it confidently described the wrong doctype entirely (Subcontracting Inward Order instead of the actually-relevant Subcontracting Order/Receipt). Nowhere near the 55-85%+ mean coverage the cloud-model arms score elsewhere in this file — `qwen3.5:4b` is not currently a usable agent for this benchmark's question difficulty, a real result about the model, not a harness defect.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-08 correction: OpenRouter has its own native Anthropic endpoint, but Claude Code CLI can't reach it directly with this model's slug

*User asked "why not call OpenRouter's `/v1/messages` directly?" — a fair challenge to the assumption above that OpenRouter needed the proxy at all. Investigated instead of defending the assumption.*

- [x] **The user was right that a direct native endpoint exists**: a raw `curl` to `https://openrouter.ai/api/v1/messages` with `model: deepseek/deepseek-v4-flash-0731` returned a clean 200 with proper Anthropic-shaped output (plain completion *and* `tool_use`, real cost/usage fields) — OpenRouter, like DeepSeek, has its own native Anthropic-Messages-API endpoint. Removed the OpenRouter entry from `harness/proxy/config.yaml` and pointed `providers.yaml` straight at it, no proxy — matching the `deepseek` entry's pattern.
- [x] **But that direct setup failed for a different, real reason**: `run.py --provider openrouter-deepseek-v4-flash` (direct) hit `api_error_status: 404` — *from inside the actual `claude` CLI*, not from OpenRouter itself (confirmed: an identical raw curl to the exact same URL+model+headers `claude` used still returns 200). Root cause: Claude Code's CLI does its own client-side handling of `--model` values containing `/` (likely provider-prefix parsing for things like Bedrock/Vertex-style model IDs) and doesn't pass an arbitrary `org/model` slug through to a custom `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` verbatim. This is a real Claude Code CLI limitation, not a proxy or OpenRouter defect.
- [x] **Net result: restored the proxy, for a different reason than originally stated**: `harness/proxy/config.yaml`'s `openrouter-deepseek-v4-flash` entry and `providers.yaml`'s proxy-routed version are both back, with corrected comments in both files explaining the *actual* reason (CLI-side slash handling, not protocol translation) — LiteLLM here is doing model-name aliasing, not Anthropic-format translation, since OpenRouter's own endpoint already speaks that format. Verified live again post-revert: real harness run, `Glob`/`Grep`/`Read`-only tool floor holds, genuine non-zero cost ($0.0077, real tokens).
- [x] **Also applied in this pass**: user asked to raise the turn cap to 30. `MAX_TURNS` in `run.py` is a single global constant (25→30) — raising it there automatically applies to every arm and every provider at once, which is the only way to do this that doesn't quietly break the "same turn limit for every arm" fairness rule the README already states. Updated the "at most 14/25 turns" mentions in `README.md`'s headline description and `PIPELINE.md`'s "Fixed config across every arm" section to 30 (narrative sections describing a specific past run at a different historical cap were left alone, since they're accurate records of what was in effect at the time, not current-state claims).
- [x] **Re-tested at the new 30-turn cap**: first attempt came back oddly empty (`result: ""`, 0 usage tokens despite a real 7.6s API call — plausibly OpenRouter routing that specific request to a flakier backend; it load-balances per-request, observed both "OpenInference" and "DeepInfra" as the `provider` field on different calls to the same model). Retried once: got a real, expensive run (24 turns, 22 tool calls, 107,140 tokens, $0.0077, 592.5s) that still ended in `"[Tool use interrupted]"` rather than a real final answer — same non-convergence pattern already documented for DeepSeek-v4-pro elsewhere in this file, now confirmed on the Flash variant too, at the new higher turn cap, not just the old one. Not re-attempted a third time — diminishing returns on a single question; this is now a characterized, not a puzzling, result.
- [ ] **Not done**: no grading of the openrouter-deepseek-v4-flash run (didn't produce a real answer to grade), no test of whether raising `MAX_TURNS` to 30 changed anything for the cloud-model arms this repo already has full results for (`claude-opus-4-8`/`claude-sonnet-5`/`deepseek-v4-pro`) — those results were all generated under the old 25-turn cap and are now technically stale relative to the new default, though not wrong for what they measured at the time.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-10 CORRECTION: a real stale-grading bug had `codegraph` reported as 4th of 5 when it was actually 1st

*User spotted a suspicious row in the HTML tool-flow report — `codegraph` Q06 showing 0.0% while every other arm scored 80%+ on the same question, next to a visibly long, well-cited, substantive answer. Investigated instead of dismissing it as noise.*

- [x] **Root cause: `grade.py`'s idempotent-skip logic is keyed on (task_id, arm) existing in `grades.json` at all — it doesn't check whether the underlying answer changed.** Every one of the 13 questions retried earlier this session (`codegraph` Q06/Q07/Q23/Q26, `graphify` Q11/Q15/Q17/Q18/Q28/Q29, `code-review-graph` Q18, `codegraph-nosem` Q19/Q21) had already been graded once *before* the retry, at 0% with reasoning literally `"The answer is empty"` — true at the time (the first attempt genuinely failed). The retry replaced the failed row in `<arm>-results.jsonl` with a real, often excellent answer, but a subsequent `grade.py` run without `--regrade` never re-graded these — it saw `(arm, task_id)` already present in `grades.json` and skipped, so `RESULTS.md`/`tool-flow-report.html` kept showing the stale "answer is empty" 0% next to what was actually a 5,000+ character, well-cited, correct-looking answer.
- [x] **Fixed surgically, not with a blanket `--regrade`** (which would have re-paid for all 151 already-correct grades): wrote a one-off Python script to remove exactly the 13 known-stale `(arm, task_id)` entries from `grades.json`, then ran `grade.py` normally — its own idempotent-skip logic then correctly re-graded only those 13 (confirmed: `"Grading 13 answers ... (138 already graded, skipped unless --regrade)"`) against a real, fresh judge pass.
- [x] **Then swept the *entire* `grades.json` for the same failure mode, not just the 13 known cases** — checked every remaining `coverage == 0.0` entry across all 5 arms × 30 questions for a mismatch between "judge said empty" and a real, non-empty answer. Found none: the 4 remaining 0% entries (`code-review-graph` Q01 — genuinely empty, the reproducible 3x failure above; `codegraph-nosem` Q20/Q23/Q25 — genuinely near-empty: a stub "I'll investigate..." that never answers, and 2 real `"[Tool use interrupted]"` hallucinated give-up cases, the same rare failure mode documented for DeepSeek Flash earlier but only 2/150 occurrences here) are all legitimate zeros, not stale grades.
- [x] **Corrected numbers, regenerated `RESULTS.md` and `tool-flow-report.html`**: `codegraph` **75.2%** (was stale-reported as 65.4%, 4th of 5) is now the *highest*-scoring arm, not the lowest-of-the-real-arms. Full corrected ranking: `codegraph` 75.2%, `codegraph-colby` 74.0%, `code-review-graph` 72.4%, `codegraph-nosem` 72.0%, `graphify` 71.1%. The spread also tightened dramatically — 71.1%-75.2% (4.1pp) vs. the stale 57.5%-74.0% (16.5pp) — because the stale-zero contamination was distributed unevenly across arms (more of `codegraph`'s and `graphify`'s retried questions were affected than `codegraph-colby`'s, which had zero retries and was never contaminated). Per-group table also flipped materially: `codegraph` now leads or ties on B (86.5%), C (83.3%), and E (89.0%), a very different picture from the earlier "leads only on C" claim.
- [x] **Process lesson, worth remembering for any future retry-then-regrade cycle in this harness**: after replacing a failed run's row via a targeted `run.py` retry, `grade.py` must either be run with `--regrade` (costly, re-pays for everything) or have the specific stale `(arm, task_id)` entries manually stripped from `grades.json` first — never assume a plain re-run of `grade.py` after a retry picks up the change. This project's harness has no built-in guard against this (grading is keyed purely on presence, not on an answer-content hash or timestamp comparison) — worth flagging as a real gap in `grade.py` itself if this pattern (retry a failed question, then re-grade) recurs.
- [ ] **Not done**: no code fix to `grade.py` itself to prevent this class of bug from recurring (e.g. hashing the answer text into the grade record and re-grading automatically when it changes) — this pass only fixed the specific contaminated data, not the underlying idempotency-keying gap that caused it.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-10 new benchmark: indexing-speed comparison across all 5 tool arms

*User asked for a separate, basic benchmark of raw indexing/build speed across the 5 tool arms — not answer quality, how long each tool takes to build its index from scratch over the same ERPNext commit, each against its own fresh clone so no tool's warm cache affects another's timing.*

- [x] **Operational decision made explicit before running anything**: timing `codegraph`'s/`codegraph-nosem`'s own indexing requires stopping the shared codegraph daemon (`codegraph index` refuses to run otherwise — confirmed via a throwaway smoke test first, which also revealed `codegraph init` silently restarts the daemon if one is already running). That daemon was serving several other live, unrelated projects (`node`, `main-be`, `django`, `deepagents`, `fe`, `lexical`, `keycloak`) at the time. Asked the user directly rather than assuming; they chose to stop it temporarily. Daemon was stopped once, both codegraph variants indexed back-to-back inside that single window, then restarted — confirmed running normally again afterward (`codegraph status`).
- [x] **`harness/bench_indexing.py`** (new, committed) — clones a fresh frappe/erpnext checkout at the pinned commit per arm (`repos/indexing-bench/<arm>/`, not nested under a further `erpnext/` subdir — a real path-convention bug was hit and fixed here: an earlier ad-hoc manual clone script used a different directory layout than the Python script assumed, causing `repo_dir()` to silently resolve to a subdirectory *inside* the erpnext checkout itself rather than the checkout root, which only surfaced when `git clone` refused to write into a non-empty directory). Times each arm's real cold-start build command (`codegraph index`, `graphify update`, `code-review-graph build`, colby's `codegraph init` — note: colby's own `index` subcommand refuses to run against an uninitialized project despite its help text implying otherwise; `init` is the actual first-build step for that tool, a second real per-tool CLI-semantics gotcha caught during this pass) and writes one combined report.
- [x] **Caught and corrected two "which run is the real cold-start" traps** while filling in file/node/symbol counts after the fact (to avoid re-running the expensive `codegraph`/`codegraph-nosem` daemon-down builds a second time just for stats): `graphify update` on an already-built `graph.json` does an incremental re-extraction, not a full rebuild — measured 56.7s vs. the genuine cold-start's 91.8s (~38% faster), and would have quietly understated `graphify`'s real indexing cost if the second, cheaper number had been kept. `codegraph-colby`'s `init` similarly skips/incrementally-updates on repeat calls against an unchanged project — 10.3s cold start vs. 0.24s/0.8s on immediate repeats. `code-review-graph build` was the one tool confirmed to **not** do this (its own "Full build:" log line and stable file/node/edge counts repeated identically across 3 separate calls) — verified rather than assumed, since assuming the wrong thing here for even one arm would have silently made a report about *build speed* actually measure *incremental-update speed* for that arm.
- [x] **Result** (`results/indexing-speed/REPORT.md`, one fresh clone per arm, single cold-start run each):

  | Arm | Wall-clock | Files | Nodes/Symbols | Edges |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | codegraph | 701.8s | 3605 | 19340 | — |
  | codegraph-nosem | 139.0s | 3605 | 19340 | — |
  | graphify | 91.8s | — | 24958 | 51779 |
  | code-review-graph | 35.1s | 3681 | 21956 | 223101 |
  | codegraph-colby | 10.3s | 3677 | 31127 | — |

  `codegraph`'s real-embedder build is by far the slowest of the 5 (701.8s) — ~5x slower than its own no-semantic/stub-embedder variant (139.0s), confirming real ONNX embedding inference (not graph extraction/parsing) is the dominant cost in this tool's indexing pipeline, and ~68x slower than the fastest arm (`codegraph-colby`, 10.3s). This is a real, load-bearing tradeoff to be upfront about, not just a QA-quality number: `codegraph`'s real-semantic indexing is drastically more expensive than every competitor tested here, in exchange for whatever retrieval-quality benefit the real embedder provides (the separate QA benchmark's own `codegraph` vs `codegraph-nosem` comparison is the place to look for whether that tradeoff is worth it — see the GLM 5.3 Flash results above, where `codegraph-nosem` actually outscored `codegraph` on quality, 79.5% vs 75.2%, at n=30, making the ~5x indexing-time cost of the real embedder look questionable on this specific evidence, though not yet validated at this file's own multi-repeat bar).
- [ ] **Not done**: only a single run per arm (explicitly flagged as indicative, not proven-reproducible, in the report itself) — indexing is less noisy than LLM QA but real variance was already observed for 2 of 5 arms on repeat calls (graphify, colby), just in a different direction (incremental speedup, not measurement noise) than what would invalidate a repeat-based average; a genuine noise-floor check (repeat cold-start runs, fresh clone each time) was not attempted given the cost (each `codegraph` cold-start alone is a ~12-minute daemon-down window). No file/symbol counts captured live for `codegraph`/`codegraph-nosem` via `codegraph status` (used the already-known QA-benchmark counts for the same commit instead, to avoid re-registering and re-indexing just for a stats readout) — these are correct by construction (identical corpus/commit) but not literally re-verified against this specific benchmark run's own index.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-11 fixed a real data-completeness gap: edge counts were missing for 3/5 arms, not actually unavailable

*User asked directly why the report showed no Edges for `codegraph`/`codegraph-nosem`/`codegraph-colby`. Investigated rather than assuming the data genuinely doesn't exist — it did, in both cases, the script just never looked in the right place.*

- [x] **`codegraph`/`codegraph-nosem`**: edge data lives in `~/.codegraph/<hash8-of-project-root>/graph.db` (per `CLAUDE.md`'s own documented data layout), not in the project's own `.codegraph/` directory (which only ever holds `config.toml`) — the original script queried nothing from there at all. `codegraph status` has no `--json`/edge-count output either, so there's no clean CLI path to this; queried `graph.db`'s `edges` table directly via `sqlite3`. Identifying *which* hash8 directory belonged to which of the two fresh benchmark clones (many other unrelated hash8 dirs exist from the user's other real projects) was done by matching exact `symbols` table row counts (19340, already known from the earlier stdout parse) to narrow to 3 candidates, then mtime ordering against the benchmark's own recorded run timings to disambiguate the 2 real candidates from a 3rd, older, unrelated match (the QA benchmark's own long-lived `repos/erpnext` index, coincidentally also 19340 symbols since it's the same corpus/commit). Result: both variants show **98,651 edges** — identical, as expected, since edge extraction comes from tree-sitter parsing, not the embedder that's the actual difference between these two arms.
- [x] **`codegraph-colby`**: its own `status --json` output already had an `edgeCount: 92325` field the whole time — the script's parsing code just never checked for that key (only looked for file/symbol-shaped keys). One-line fix.
- [x] **Fixed properly in `harness/bench_indexing.py` itself, not just patched in the report**: added a `newest_codegraph_data_dir()` + `count_edges()` helper (explicitly documented as safe *only* because this script runs one `codegraph index` at a time with the daemon stopped throughout — nothing else can be touching `~/.codegraph/` concurrently, which is what makes "most recently modified `graph.db`" an unambiguous identifier) to `bench_codegraph_variant()`, and added the missing `edgeCount` key check to `bench_codegraph_colby()`. A future re-run of this script will capture edges correctly the first time, not require this same recovery investigation again.
- [x] **Final, complete report** (`results/indexing-speed/REPORT.md`, `README.md` both updated): `codegraph` 98,651 edges, `codegraph-nosem` 98,651 edges (identical, confirms edge count is embedder-independent as expected), `graphify` 51,779, `code-review-graph` 223,101, `codegraph-colby` 92,325. All 5 arms now have complete files/nodes-symbols/edges data, no more placeholder dashes.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-11 tried and REVERTED: colby-inspired file co-location boost regressed the benchmark badly

*Following the colby deep-dive above, user asked to implement the "file-level co-location boost" idea (candidate score boosted when multiple query-matched direct hits share a file) and validate it against a real GLM 5.3 Flash re-run of the `codegraph-nosem` ERPNext arm — chosen specifically because it isolates the lexical+graph pipeline from embedding noise.*

- [x] **Implemented** in `codegraph-core/src/retrieval/pipeline.rs`'s `rerank()`: a `direct_hits_per_file` map counting distinct direct-hit symbols per file, with an additive `COLOCATION_BOOST_PER_EXTRA_MATCH = 0.15` per extra co-located direct hit, applied unconditionally (both real-embedding and degraded modes, unlike the existing `graph_credit`/`test_penalty` terms which are mode-gated). Passed `cargo build --workspace`, `cargo test --workspace` (36/36 test groups), and `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` clean before going anywhere near a live re-run.
- [x] **Could not validate against the historically-cited lexical/keycloak figures** (`llm-task-eval.py`'s "anthropic" backend, the one that produced the 100%/80% numbers in this file's own earlier history) — no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` in this environment. The script's free `EVAL_BACKEND=ollama` mode exists but generates answers with a different (local, weaker) model, so its numbers aren't comparable to the cited baseline — judged the time cost not worth a low-confidence signal and went straight to the explicit, real validation the user asked for instead. **In hindsight this was the wrong call** — skipping even a low-confidence cheap regression check meant the next signal came from a $1+ real ERPNext run instead of a free one, on a change that turned out to badly regress. Next retrieval-algorithm change: run *some* free/cheap check first even if imperfect, before spending on the expensive one.
- [x] **Real ERPNext GLM 5.3 Flash re-run, full 30 questions, `codegraph-nosem` arm, after a clean daemon-stop/binary-swap/restart cycle**: mean coverage **collapsed from 79.5% to 53.5%** (-26pp). 11 of 30 questions dropped to exactly 0.0% (from real prior scores of 35.7%-91.7%) — all 11 hit the 30-turn cap with an empty final answer despite heavy real tool use (38-47 tool calls each), a qualitatively different and far worse failure pattern than anything else observed with this model/arm combination all session (including the earlier documented "intermittent" `code-review-graph` Q01 case, which was 1 question, not 11 simultaneously).
- [x] **Root-caused, not just observed, before reverting**: called `find_relevant_code` live against `erpnext-nosem` with one of the newly-failing questions' actual intent text. Top-2 results both came from the same file and scored **above 1.0** (1.039, 0.926) — outside the normal roughly-0.3-0.9 direct-hit range this file's own `TEST_FILE_PENALTY` comment documents. The boost is additive and unbounded (`0.15 × extra_co-located_count`, no cap), so a file with several direct hits driven by common/generic query terms (not genuine relevance) gets a large, uncapped score inflation that can bury the actual best single-hit answer — exactly the failure mode a properly-swept, capped, or normalized version would need to guard against, and exactly what shipping an unvalidated magic constant (the mistake this file's own history already warned against, see `TEST_FILE_PENALTY`'s sweep-documented sizing) produces.
- [x] **Reverted immediately via `git checkout -- crates/codegraph-core/src/retrieval/pipeline.rs`** (safe — no other pending edits in that file), rebuilt, redeployed the clean binary to the live daemon, restarted, confirmed `erpnext`/`erpnext-nosem` back to `fresh` with correct counts. Restored the benchmark's own data to the pre-experiment good state: `codegraph-nosem-results.jsonl`/`ledger.json`/relevant `grades.json` entries reset from the preserved `*.BEFORE-colocation-boost.*` copies made before the experimental run; the failed run's own data preserved as `*.FAILED-colocation-boost-experiment.*` for the record, not deleted. `RESULTS.md`/`tool-flow-report.html` regenerated — confirmed back to the correct 79.5% mean.
- [ ] **Not done / open**: the underlying idea (file-level co-location as a relevance signal) is not necessarily wrong — colby's own version works for colby, at colby's own score scale, with whatever cap/normalization it has that this implementation didn't replicate faithfully. A disciplined next attempt would need: a cap on the boost (e.g. per this file's own established pattern, sweep a capped/normalized version — perhaps `boost.min(some_max)` or scaled by `1/candidates.len()` — against neighboring values), and a real regression check (lexical/keycloak, even via the free ollama path as an imperfect-but-nonzero signal) *before* spending on a real ERPNext re-run, not after. Not attempted in this pass — reverting and reporting honestly took priority over immediately trying a second, better-tuned version.

## Plan 19 — Public Release Hardening (indexing speed, language parity, cleanup, logo, README)

*Full plan: `plans/19-public-release-hardening.md`. User asked (2026-09-15) for five things together: faster indexing (measured against competitors, not vibes), language count parity with `graphify`, a benchmark re-run, a pre-public-release cleanup pass, and a logo. Orientation + two measurement forks (binary-size bloat probe, indexing head-to-head) done before any code changed, per this file's own established rigor bar.*

- [x] **Decisions locked in with the user**: binary size — investigate composition first (done, see below) rather than guessing between raising the gate or feature-gating; language scope — match graphify's full ~30-extension surface (config/markup formats included, not just the ~15 real-programming-language subset); paid ERPNext re-run — explicitly skipped (wouldn't reflect this pass's changes anyway; ERPNext is Python-only, lexical is TS/JS, keycloak is Java), free `scripts/llm-task-eval.py` used as the regression gate instead.
- [x] **Finding 1 — binary size root cause identified**: current release binary (already built with `strip = true`, so this is the real shipped size) is **66MB**, already 16MB over the documented ≤50MB gate *before* adding any languages. `cargo bloat --release --crates -n 40` shows `ort_sys` (ONNX Runtime FFI bindings) alone at 11.1MB / 38.3% of the `.text` section — by far the single largest crate — while all 15 tree-sitter grammars combined are bucketed inside an "And 270 more crates" line totaling only 2.5MB / 8.8%. Pre-link `.rlib` sizes corroborate: `ort_sys` ~74.7MB raw vs. all 15 grammars summed ~41MB raw. **Conclusion: the grammars are not what's blowing the size budget — `ort_sys` + the LanceDB/Arrow/DataFusion/AWS-SDK stack is.** Recommendation to the user (not yet actioned, needs explicit sign-off since it changes a documented gate): raise the ship gate to reflect reality rather than trying to slim `ort_sys`/Lance, which is a much bigger separate undertaking.
- [x] **Finding 2 — indexing speed gap measured and root-caused, first fix shipped**. Same repo (`benchmarks/dataset/repos/erpnext`), same machine, sequential (not concurrent — avoided CPU-contention noise) fresh runs: `codegraph index --fresh` real-embeddings 740.18s (matches README's documented 701.8s within noise); `codegraph index --fresh` no-semantic/graph-only 138.88s (matches README's documented 139.0s almost exactly — reproducible); `graphify update .` (AST-only, its own zero-LLM fairness rule) 82.18s while processing **more** files (4996 vs. our 3605 — it parses the 4230 `.json` doctype files this repo has and we currently skip entirely). Throughput: codegraph no-semantic ≈26 files/sec vs. graphify ≈61 files/sec — **graphify is ~2.3x faster per file while covering more file types**, confirming the user's complaint with real numbers, not vibes.
- [x] **Root cause found via CPU-utilization ratio**: the no-semantic fresh run showed `138.88s real` vs. `148.01s` combined user+sys CPU — **~1.07x, i.e. essentially no parallelism** — despite `extract_many()` being fully parallel (rayon, memory-budget-aware pool). (Contrast: the real-embedding run showed ~5.6x, consistent with the documented ~6.1x parallel-extraction figure, so parallelism clearly *can* engage on this machine — it just wasn't during the graph-only phase.) Read `full_index_graph_blocking` (`crates/codegraph-watcher/src/worker.rs`): each `FULL_INDEX_CHUNK_SIZE=200`-file chunk's disk reads were a **plain serial `for` loop** ahead of the already-parallel `extract_many()` call — for ~19 chunks across 3605 files, this serial I/O step was very likely dominating wall time while CPU sat mostly idle.
- [x] **Fix shipped and verified**: parallelized the per-chunk read+checksum step in `full_index_graph_blocking` with `rayon::par_iter`, routed through the same bounded `pool` already used for `extract_many` (so I/O concurrency stays capped by the existing memory-budget-aware `pool_size`, not unbounded). Kept the diff minimal — first pass over-applied `cargo fmt` to the whole crate (194-line unrelated reformatting of pre-existing manually-aligned code across 5 files); caught before committing anything, reverted via `git show HEAD:<path>`-sourced restores + `cp`, and the fix reapplied by hand onto the pristine files so the shipped diff is exactly the read-parallelization change (`crates/codegraph-watcher/src/worker.rs`, +1 import, ~15 changed lines). `cargo test --workspace` passes (full suite, not just the touched crate), `cargo clippy -p codegraph-watcher -- -D warnings` clean. Release binary rebuilt, re-measured fresh on the same erpnext corpus **3 times** (repeat bar, since this file has twice caught a self-reported number not surviving re-measurement): **138.88s → 90.90s / 88.22s / 87.36s** (mean ≈88.8s, ~36% faster), identical output all 3 runs (3605 files, 19,340 symbols — pure speedup, no behavior change). CPU-utilization ratio improved from ~1.07x to ~1.8x, confirming the diagnosis. Closes most of the gap to graphify: was ~1.7x slower, now ~1.08x slower, while codegraph still does strictly more per-file work (SQLite graph writes, `resolve_edges`, FTS indexing) than graphify's pure AST pass. **Caveat, measured**: this ~36% figure is the no-semantic (graph-only) path only. The default real-embedding path was also directly re-measured fresh: **740.18s → 731.03s, ~1.2% faster** — real but small, since that path spends the large majority of its wall time in ONNX inference, which this fix doesn't touch. Do not cite "34-37% faster indexing" without specifying no-semantic mode.

Caught 2 real bugs this way: (1) Fortran's `@def` spans were initially anchored to the `function_statement`/`subroutine_statement` header line, not the outer `function`/`subroutine` wrapper that contains the body — `extract_edges` resolves calls to their enclosing symbol by byte-range, so every in-body call was silently resolving to nothing; fixed by anchoring to the wrapper node. (2) Verilog's `X Y(...)` module-instantiation syntax is genuinely ambiguous without a symbol table — verified directly that it only parses as `module_instantiation` (vs. `checker_instantiation`/`udp_instantiation`) when the instantiating module's declaration precedes the instantiated one in file order; documented as a real limitation in the code, not silently accepted as a query bug.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-17 (same day): the real no-semantic bottleneck found — LanceDB per-file delete calls, 79.5s of a 93.8s run

*User asked why `codegraph-nosem` was still slow despite the earlier read-parallelization fix, explicitly deprioritizing the real-embedder path (a CoreML investigation was offered and declined in favor of this). Proper per-phase wall-clock profiling (temporary `eprintln!` timers, not just the `real`-vs-`user+sys` ratio technique used for the first fix) found the real answer.*

- [x] **Profiled, not guessed**: `full_index_graph` 14.4s, `run_scip_enrichment` 0.02s, `embed_backlog` 82.8s (88% of total). Within `embed_backlog`: `build_chunks` 3.1s, `embed_chunks` (zero-vector stub) 0.01s, **`vector_upsert+fts` 79.5s** — the actual bottleneck, by a wide margin.
- [x] **Root cause, found by reading `VectorDb::upsert` directly**: it issued **one `.delete()` call per unique file_path in every 32-file batch** — ~3605 individual LanceDB delete transactions for this corpus (each a real transaction: new manifest version, real I/O) before the batch's single `.add()` call. Fires on every file even on a `--fresh` run into an empty table, where every delete matches zero rows — the cost is transactional overhead, not proportional to matched rows.
- [x] **Fixed**: collapsed the per-file `delete()` loop into one `file_path IN (...)` predicate per batch (`crates/codegraph-store/src/vector_db.rs`, `VectorDb::upsert`) — ~3605 delete transactions down to ~113. `cargo test -p codegraph-store` 42/42 (incl. `vectordb_upsert_replaces_old_file_vectors`, the test most directly exercising this path), `cargo test --workspace` and `cargo clippy --workspace -- -D warnings` both clean.
- [x] **Verified, 3 repeated fresh runs**: `codegraph-nosem` **26.15s / 25.32s / 24.51s (mean ≈25.3s)** — a further **~3.5x** on top of the already-fixed ≈88.8s. Combined with the first fix, the total improvement from the original pre-Plan-19 number is **138.88s → ≈25.3s, ~5.5x**. Also confirmed on the real-embedder path (same shared code, one confirmation run): **788.3s → 701.2s** (~11%, smaller proportionally since ONNX inference still dominates there, but a real ~87s absolute win for free).
- [x] **Reorders the 5-arm ranking, not just narrows the gap**: `codegraph-nosem` was 4th of 5 arms (only beating real-embedder `codegraph` itself); at ≈25.3s it's now **2nd-fastest**, beating `code-review-graph` (36.9s) and `graphify` (64.4s), behind only `codegraph-colby` (11.3s). Full writeup, including why the first profiling technique (CPU-utilization ratio) didn't catch this I/O-bound bottleneck, in `plans/19-public-release-hardening.md`'s Finding 2b and `benchmarks/dataset/README.md`'s indexing-speed section.
- [ ] **Not done**: the temporary CoreML investigation the user asked about first was explicitly deprioritized in favor of this fix, per their own redirect — still open if real-embedder indexing speed becomes a priority again.
- [ ] **Not investigated, flagged only**: README.md's benchmark table row "Agent task accuracy (Config B) | 98–100%" (also quoted verbatim in `docs/claude-code-hook.md:35`) traces to `bench-reports/lexical-find-relevant-code-2026-08-03.md`, which does mention "Config B" but reports a different metric there ("avg 0.88/3.0", plus separate 100% category pass-rate rows) — the exact "98–100%" figure isn't verbatim in that file or anywhere else searched. Left as-is rather than guessing a replacement number; worth someone with access to the original benchmark run confirming or re-sourcing it.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-18: "fix all" pass on the remaining open items from Plan 19 (binary size, `extract_incremental`, `resolve_edges`, stale docs)

*User asked to fix everything still open from the Plan 19 wrap-up, explicitly deciding not to worry about binary size (drop the languages-vs-size tradeoff, keep all 13 Phase A languages).*

- [x] **Binary size resolved by decision, not code**: ship gate raised 50MB → 200MB in CLAUDE.md, with the reasoning (Plan 19's actual 115.5MB post-Phase-A measurement) recorded inline so a future reader doesn't wonder where 200MB came from.
- [x] **`extract_incremental()` fixed and wired in — was a non-functional stub, not just "unused"**: reading the function found its own comment admitting it always did a full parse regardless of the `tree_cache` argument ("the old tree is only useful when the caller has applied edit annotations... without them, incremental reuse produces incorrect results" — and nothing ever applied them). Implemented real incremental parsing: `compute_input_edit()` diffs old vs. new source via common-prefix/suffix trim into a `tree_sitter::InputEdit`, `byte_to_point()` converts offsets to the row/column `tree_sitter::Point`s the edit needs, applied via `Tree::edit()` before `Parser::parse(new_source, Some(&old_tree))`. Moved the cache from an external caller-supplied `DashMap<PathBuf, Tree>` (awkward — no source-bytes tracking, so no diffing was possible with the old signature at all) to an internal `Extractor::incremental_cache: DashMap<PathBuf, (Vec<u8>, Tree)>`, with a new `invalidate_incremental_cache()` for the watcher to call on file delete. Wired into `IndexWorker::index_path_graph_only` (the live single-file save path), replacing the old always-full `extract()` call — `extract_many` (parallel full-index path) intentionally left untouched, since a fresh parser per file there already outperforms any per-file cache reuse.
- [x] **7 new correctness tests** in `extraction.rs`: append, mid-file insert, deletion, byte-identical-no-op, and cache-invalidation-forces-fresh-parse, each asserting incremental and full-reparse produce identical symbol sets — not just "it doesn't crash." All pass, plus the full existing watcher integration suite (`modify_file_only_reindexes_that_file`, `create_file_appears_in_sqlite_and_lancedb`, `delete_file_removes_symbols_from_both_stores`, `rapid_saves_p95_latency_and_no_duplicates`) confirming the live-save path end-to-end, not just the parser unit tests.
- [x] **Measured, not assumed**: ~1.3x speedup on a real 6,400-line ERPNext test file, for both an append and a small mid-file edit (temporary perf tests, removed before landing — not shipped). Honest about the ceiling: the win is capped by symbol/edge extraction re-running over the *whole* tree regardless of how localized the parse-level edit was (tree-sitter's incrementality applies to parsing, not to query execution) — a real, separate, not-yet-attempted further opportunity (`Tree::changed_ranges()`) if this needs to go further later.
- [x] **`resolve_edges()`/chunk-pipelining profiled, found not worth doing**: added temporary per-phase timers (removed before landing) and ran a fresh no-semantic index. Real breakdown: `t_read=2.49s`, `t_extract=7.93s` (already parallel, dominant cost), `t_write=2.70s`, `resolve_edges=0.37s`, `synthesis=0.26s` — both `resolve_edges` and `synthesis` are already negligible, contrary to the concern flagged when they were deferred in Finding 2. Chunk-to-chunk pipelining's theoretical ceiling is only the write time (~2.7s, ~10% of the ~26.5s total) since write is much smaller than read+extract per chunk — concluded the win doesn't justify the concurrent-SQLite-write correctness risk of actually building it. **Deliberately not implemented** — a measured "no" is still a real outcome, not a skipped task.
- [x] **Stale "98–100% Config B" figure fixed with a sourced replacement, not just removed**: traced it as far as a prior docs audit had (unverifiable, no source found) and independently re-confirmed that with a fresh search. Re-ran the actual free (`EVAL_BACKEND=string`, no LLM cost) 40-task lexical retrieval check against the live daemon to get a *current*, verified number: **100% (40/40)**, matching the checklist's own well-documented multi-run-verified history. Updated README's Benchmarks table row and `docs/claude-code-hook.md`'s separate (and even less sourced — a "~75% without the instruction" comparison with no experiment behind it at all) claim to both cite this real, freshly-verified figure instead.

### Follow-up — 2026-09-18: JSON support added (Plan 19 Phase C) — closes most of the file-count gap vs. graphify

*User noticed codegraph was still indexing far fewer files than graphify on erpnext and asked to close it. Root cause already known from Plan 19 Finding 3: erpnext has 4230 `.json` files vs. 2867 `.py` — more JSON than Python — and none of it was indexed.*

- [x] **Added `JsonLang`** (`tree-sitter-json` 0.24.8, ABI 14, clean at latest — checked directly, not assumed). Deliberately generic rather than tuned to ERPNext/Frappe's doctype JSON convention: only a file's **top-level object keys** become symbols (kind `Const`), anchored via `(document (object (pair key: (string (string_content) @name)))  @def)` — verified directly against a real doctype-shaped snippet (`{"name":..., "fields":[{"fieldname":..., "nested":{"deep":...}}], "actions":[]}`) that nested keys (`fieldname`, `nested`, `deep`) are correctly excluded, only `name`/`fields`/`actions` extracted. No call or import query — JSON has neither, and no single convention (like `$ref`) generalizes across arbitrary JSON files without overfitting to one dialect. 3 new tests in `lang_coverage.rs` (top-level-only scoping, array-rooted file produces zero symbols, no call/import edges), all passing alongside the full 87-test suite.
- [x] **Registered-language count: 31** (30 + json). `Language::Json` added to `codegraph-core`, registered in `LanguageRegistry`, added to `default_languages()`.
- [x] **Real, measured impact on erpnext**: fresh no-semantic index went from **3605 → 7825 files** (+4220, essentially the full JSON count) and **19,340 → 64,704 symbols** (+45,364). This now matches or exceeds graphify's own 4996-file coverage on the same repo. Indexing time correspondingly rose from ≈25.3s to 87.4s — a real, expected cost of real new work, not a regression (still far below the pre-Plan-19 138.88s baseline).
- [x] **Regression-checked, not assumed safe**: re-ran the free (`EVAL_BACKEND=string`, no LLM cost) 40-task lexical retrieval check after the JSON-inclusive re-index — still **100% (40/40)**, no change from adding a new candidate-pool-competing language.
